# Daily Summary: 2025-05-11

### The Aloe Family Recipe for Open and Specialized Healthcare LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04388v1)
- **Authors**: Dario Garcia-Gasulla, Jordi Bayarri-Planas, Ashwin Kumar Gururajan, Enrique Lopez-Cuena, Adrian Tormos, Daniel Hinjos, Pablo Bernabeu-Perez, Anna Arias-Duart, Pablo Agustin Martin-Torres, Marta Gonzalez-Mallo, Sergio Alvarez-Napagao, Eduard Ayguadé-Parra, Ulises Cortés
- **Abstract**: Purpose: With advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) for healthcare, the need arises for competitive open-source models to protect the public interest. This work contributes to the field of open medical LLMs by optimizing key stages of data preprocessing and training, while showing how to improve model safety (through DPO) and efficacy (through RAG). The evaluation methodology used, which includes four different types of tests, defines a new standard for the field. The resultant models, shown to be competitive with the best private alternatives, are released with a permisive license. Methods: Building on top of strong base models like Llama 3.1 and Qwen 2.5, Aloe Beta uses a custom dataset to enhance public data with synthetic Chain of Thought examples. The models undergo alignment with Direct Preference Optimization, emphasizing ethical and policy-aligned performance in the presence of jailbreaking attacks. Evaluation includes close-ended, open-ended, safety and human assessments, to maximize the reliability of results. Results: Recommendations are made across the entire pipeline, backed by the solid performance of the Aloe Family. These models deliver competitive performance across healthcare benchmarks and medical fields, and are often preferred by healthcare professionals. On bias and toxicity, the Aloe Beta models significantly improve safety, showing resilience to unseen jailbreaking attacks. For a responsible release, a detailed risk assessment specific to healthcare is attached to the Aloe Family models. Conclusion: The Aloe Beta models, and the recipe that leads to them, are a significant contribution to the open-source medical LLM field, offering top-of-the-line performance while maintaining high ethical requirements. This work sets a new standard for developing and reporting aligned LLMs in healthcare.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "The Aloe Family Recipe for Open and Specialized Healthcare LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Aloe Beta," a family of open-source large language models (LLMs) tailored for healthcare applications.  The authors build upon existing open-source base models (Llama 3.1 and Qwen 2.5) and fine-tune them using a custom dataset that combines public medical data with synthetically generated Chain-of-Thought examples. The paper emphasizes safety and ethical alignment through Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to mitigate jailbreaking attacks and improve overall ethical performance. The authors present a multi-stage training process involving supervised fine-tuning (SFT), model merging (DARE-TIES), and model alignment.  The models are rigorously evaluated using a comprehensive methodology that includes close-ended, open-ended, safety, and human assessments. The resulting Aloe Beta models demonstrate competitive performance across various healthcare benchmarks, often preferred by healthcare professionals, and exhibit improved safety compared to baseline models.  The paper also releases the Aloe Family models and datasets under permissive licenses, promoting open research and accessibility. Finally, a detailed risk assessment specific to the healthcare domain is attached to the Aloe Family models to demonstrate responsible release.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive approach to building and evaluating open healthcare LLMs. While individual components, such as fine-tuning on medical data, model merging, and safety alignment, are not entirely new, the authors combine these techniques in a unique and optimized pipeline specifically tailored for the healthcare domain. The creation of a custom dataset with synthetic CoT examples and the detailed risk assessment further contribute to the paper's novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of open medical LLMs. By releasing high-performing, open-source models and datasets, the authors promote transparency, accessibility, and reproducibility in a domain where closed-source solutions have traditionally dominated. The models' competitive performance against private alternatives and the emphasis on safety and ethical alignment are crucial steps towards building trustworthy and beneficial AI tools for healthcare. The comprehensive evaluation methodology, including human assessments and safety metrics, sets a new standard for developing and reporting aligned LLMs in healthcare.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The paper covers all key stages of LLM development, from data curation and training to evaluation and risk assessment.
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** The evaluation methodology is thorough and multifaceted, encompassing both automated and human assessments.
    *   **Openness and reproducibility:** The release of models, datasets, and code promotes open research and allows others to build upon the authors' work.
    *   **Emphasis on safety and ethics:** The paper prioritizes safety and ethical alignment, addressing crucial concerns in the healthcare domain.
    *   **Competitive performance:** The Aloe Beta models demonstrate strong performance across various healthcare benchmarks and medical fields.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope of human evaluation:** The human evaluation is limited in scale due to the cost of expert hours.
    *   **Potential for data contamination:** The use of public datasets raises concerns about potential data contamination, although the authors take steps to mitigate this risk.
    *   **Ongoing challenges in LLM evaluation:** As acknowledged in the paper, drawing reliable conclusions from LLM evaluation remains challenging due to the current limitations of LLM evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to have a significant influence on the field of medical LLMs. By providing open-source alternatives to closed-source models, the authors empower researchers and practitioners to develop and deploy AI solutions for healthcare more transparently and ethically. The paper's comprehensive evaluation methodology and emphasis on safety and ethics will likely shape future research in this area.

**Score: 8.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a significant advancement in the creation and evaluation of open-source healthcare LLMs. Its strengths lie in its comprehensive approach, rigorous evaluation, and commitment to transparency and ethical alignment. While the paper has some limitations, such as the limited scope of human evaluation and the potential for data contamination, its overall contribution to the field is substantial. The release of high-quality open-source models and datasets, along with a detailed risk assessment, will enable others to build upon this work and accelerate the development of trustworthy and beneficial AI tools for healthcare.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Large Means Left: Political Bias in Large Language Models Increases with Their Number of Parameters
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04393v1)
- **Authors**: David Exler, Mark Schutera, Markus Reischl, Luca Rettenberger
- **Abstract**: With the increasing prevalence of artificial intelligence, careful evaluation of inherent biases needs to be conducted to form the basis for alleviating the effects these predispositions can have on users. Large language models (LLMs) are predominantly used by many as a primary source of information for various topics. LLMs frequently make factual errors, fabricate data (hallucinations), or present biases, exposing users to misinformation and influencing opinions. Educating users on their risks is key to responsible use, as bias, unlike hallucinations, cannot be caught through data verification. We quantify the political bias of popular LLMs in the context of the recent vote of the German Bundestag using the score produced by the Wahl-O-Mat. This metric measures the alignment between an individual's political views and the positions of German political parties. We compare the models' alignment scores to identify factors influencing their political preferences. Doing so, we discover a bias toward left-leaning parties, most dominant in larger LLMs. Also, we find that the language we use to communicate with the models affects their political views. Additionally, we analyze the influence of a model's origin and release date and compare the results to the outcome of the recent vote of the Bundestag. Our results imply that LLMs are prone to exhibiting political bias. Large corporations with the necessary means to develop LLMs, thus, knowingly or unknowingly, have a responsibility to contain these biases, as they can influence each voter's decision-making process and inform public opinion in general and at scale.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates political biases in large language models (LLMs) concerning the recent German Bundestag vote. The authors use the "Wahl-O-Mat," a tool measuring the alignment between individual political views and party positions, to quantify the political bias of several popular LLMs. They compare the models' alignment scores to identify factors that might influence their political preferences. The study reveals a tendency towards left-leaning political parties, especially in larger LLMs. The language used in communicating with the models also appears to impact their political views.  Furthermore, the study explores the influence of a model's origin, release date, and size on its political leaning. The findings suggest that LLMs are susceptible to political bias, thus raising concerns about their potential influence on public opinion and voter decision-making.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Relevant and Timely:** The topic is highly relevant given the increasing reliance on LLMs for information and the potential for bias to influence public discourse. The investigation into political bias is particularly important in democratic societies.
    * **Quantifiable Metric:** The use of the Wahl-O-Mat provides a quantifiable metric for measuring political alignment, which allows for objective comparisons between different models.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study examines various factors that could potentially influence political bias in LLMs, including model size, origin, release date, and input language.
    * **Replication and Extension:**  The authors build upon previous work in this area (Rettenberger et al.) by including new LLMs, considering the model's origin, and quantifying the LLMs' left/right positioning, contributing to the ongoing scrutiny and transparency of LLMs.
    * **Open-Source Models Focus:** Using open-source models increases the accessibility and reproducibility of the research, fostering further investigation by the community.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study focuses solely on the German political context and the Wahl-O-Mat metric. While relevant to Germany, the findings may not be generalizable to other countries or political systems. The Wahl-O-Mat metric, while useful, may not capture the full spectrum of political views.
    * **Causality vs. Correlation:** The paper identifies correlations between model characteristics (size, release date) and political bias, but it doesn't establish causal relationships.  It is difficult to determine whether these factors directly cause the bias or whether they are merely correlated with other underlying factors (e.g., training data bias).
    * **Influence on Voting Undetermined:** The study acknowledges it cannot definitively determine the degree to which the observed bias impacted voting decisions during the Bundestagswahl. It's important to note that the political bias may have been mitigated by a combination of factors or by the lack of extensive LLM use in political discussions.
    * **Neutral Interpretation**: The 'Neutral' results are a bit ambiguous and might reflect a lack of understanding or inability to formulate a stance on the issue, not necessarily a truly neutral political position. The authors could analyze instances of these 'Neutral' answers further to derive a more informed interpretation.
    * **Oversimplification of Political Positions:** Reducing political positions to a linear left/right spectrum, although necessary for quantification, oversimplifies the complexity of political ideologies and nuances.

* **Novelty and Significance:**
    The study is novel in its inclusion of newer LLMs (DeepSeek R1, SimpleScaling S1) and its attempt to assess the influence of the model's origin (American vs. Chinese LLMs). While it confirms existing observations about left-leaning bias in LLMs, it extends the analysis by considering additional factors and newer models. The research contributes to the growing body of literature on AI bias and its potential impact on society.

* **Potential Influence:**
    The study can raise awareness among developers, policymakers, and the public about the potential for political bias in LLMs.  It can also inform the development of strategies to mitigate bias in LLMs and promote responsible AI development. The results emphasize the importance of continued scrutiny and transparency in the development and deployment of LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper tackles a significant and timely issue with a reasonable methodology. It builds upon existing research and expands the analysis by considering new models and factors, which earns credit for novelty. However, its limitations in scope, potential for generalizability beyond the German context, and inability to establish causal relationships hinder its overall impact. It is a valuable contribution to the field, prompting further investigation but falling short of being a groundbreaking or transformative piece of research. The rigorous justification of the scoring highlights both the merit and the shortcomings of this work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### YABLoCo: Yet Another Benchmark for Long Context Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04406v1)
- **Authors**: Aidar Valeev, Roman Garaev, Vadim Lomshakov, Irina Piontkovskaya, Vladimir Ivanov, Israel Adewuyi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models demonstrate the ability to solve various programming tasks, including code generation. Typically, the performance of LLMs is measured on benchmarks with small or medium-sized context windows of thousands of lines of code. At the same time, in real-world software projects, repositories can span up to millions of LoC. This paper closes this gap by contributing to the long context code generation benchmark (YABLoCo). The benchmark featured a test set of 215 functions selected from four large repositories with thousands of functions. The dataset contained metadata of functions, contexts of the functions with different levels of dependencies, docstrings, functions bodies, and call graphs for each repository. This paper presents three key aspects of the contribution. First, the benchmark aims at function body generation in large repositories in C and C++, two languages not covered by previous benchmarks. Second, the benchmark contains large repositories from 200K to 2,000K LoC. Third, we contribute a scalable evaluation pipeline for efficient computing of the target metrics and a tool for visual analysis of generated code. Overall, these three aspects allow for evaluating code generation in large repositories in C and C++.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces YABLoCo, a new benchmark for long-context code generation, specifically targeting C and C++ repositories.  It addresses the gap in existing benchmarks that primarily focus on Python and Java and often lack the scale to evaluate code generation in realistic, large-scale codebases (hundreds of thousands to millions of lines of code). The benchmark includes 215 functions from four large open-source projects, along with metadata like function bodies, docstrings, signatures, call graphs, and test coverage information. The authors also provide an evaluation pipeline for calculating common metrics like Pass@k, Exact Match, and Edit Similarity and a visual analysis tool. They experiment with CodeLlama, DeepSeekCoder, and GPT-4 baselines with and without an "oracle" context (all functions called by the target function).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper has moderate novelty. While existing benchmarks address repository-level code generation, YABLoCo distinguishes itself by focusing on C/C++, a largely neglected area, and by using truly large repositories.  The creation of an associated evaluation pipeline also adds value.  However, the evaluation metrics themselves (Pass@k, Exact Match, Edit Similarity) are standard. The idea of an "oracle" context, while interesting, is not entirely novel as it's effectively retrieval augmented generation, and other papers have explored context in code generation.
* **Significance:** The significance is moderate.  The C/C++ community will benefit from a dedicated benchmark for evaluating code generation models.  The size of the repositories used is a clear step towards more realistic evaluation scenarios. The authors' analysis of different LLMs with and without context is useful. However, the experiments are limited in scope, and the results don't radically alter the current understanding of LLMs' performance on code. The benchmark provides a valuable resource for future research.
* **Strengths:**
    * Addresses a gap in the existing benchmark landscape by focusing on C/C++ and large repositories.
    * Provides a comprehensive dataset with useful metadata (call graphs, test coverage).
    * Offers an evaluation pipeline for reproducible results.
    * The visual analysis tool aids in qualitative assessment.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * Limited experimentation with LLMs and context types. The exploration of different contexts is relatively basic (oracle context only). More sophisticated retrieval or context selection techniques could have been explored.
    * Potential data leakage due to the inclusion of two repositories present in pre-training data. Though the authors acknowledge this and use Exact Match, it is still a concern.
    *  Reliance on existing tests for Pass@k. As the authors admit, writing new tests would increase the quality of the pass@k metric.
    * Lack of deeper error analysis of generated code.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable resource to the code generation community, particularly those working with C/C++ and concerned with long-context handling.  While the core ideas are not entirely groundbreaking, the meticulous data collection, benchmark creation, and associated tools make it a worthwhile contribution. The limitations outlined prevent it from being a truly transformative work, especially the limited depth of LLM experimentation.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### OBLIVIATE: Robust and Practical Machine Unlearning for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04416v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyu Xu, Minxin Du, Qingqing Ye, Haibo Hu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) trained over extensive corpora risk memorizing sensitive, copyrighted, or toxic content. To address this, we propose OBLIVIATE, a robust unlearning framework that removes targeted data while preserving model utility. The framework follows a structured process: extracting target tokens, building retain sets, and fine-tuning with a tailored loss function comprising three components -- masking, distillation, and world fact. Using low-rank adapters (LoRA), it ensures efficiency without compromising unlearning quality. We conduct experiments on multiple datasets, including the Harry Potter series, WMDP, and TOFU, using a comprehensive suite of metrics: forget quality (new document-level memorization score), model utility, and fluency. Results demonstrate its effectiveness in resisting membership inference attacks, minimizing the impact on retained data, and maintaining robustness across diverse scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OBLIVIATE: Robust and Practical Machine Unlearning for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OBLIVIATE, a new machine unlearning framework specifically designed for large language models (LLMs). Addressing concerns about LLMs memorizing sensitive, copyrighted, or toxic content, OBLIVIATE aims to effectively remove targeted data while preserving model utility (performance on retained data and fluency). The framework employs a structured three-step process: (1) extracting target tokens using GPT-4o, (2) building a retain set of generic, other-style, and world-fact documents, and (3) fine-tuning the model using a tailored loss function with masking, distillation, and world fact components.  The method uses LoRA for efficient fine-tuning and is evaluated on multiple datasets (Harry Potter, WMDP, TOFU) using metrics for forget quality, model utility, and fluency. Results show the framework's effectiveness in resisting membership inference attacks, minimizing the impact on retained data, and maintaining robustness across diverse scenarios. A document-level memorization metric is also introduced.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The paper combines existing techniques (LoRA, distillation) but innovates by structuring them into a specific framework for LLM unlearning, particularly with the carefully constructed loss function and GPT-4o-based token extraction. This structured approach is presented as novel, however the individual components are not.
    *   The introduction of the document-level memorization metric (DRMA) is a novel contribution. Existing metrics often focus on token-level or instance-level memorization.  DRMA aims to capture more holistic memorization behavior across documents, which is important in assessing unlearning efficacy.
    *   The selective and context-aware unlearning ability, achieved through combining masking, distillation, and world fact losses, could be considered novel. It offers a way to unlearn sensitive information in harmful contexts without indiscriminately erasing unrelated knowledge.
    *   Using GPT-4o for token identification offers improved adaptability compared to traditional Named Entity Recognition or statistical methods, particularly for complex target concepts, making the approach practical.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper addresses a significant and timely issue: the need for effective and practical machine unlearning techniques for LLMs. As LLMs are increasingly deployed in various applications, the ability to remove sensitive or unwanted information becomes crucial for compliance with regulations and ethical considerations.
    *   The framework claims robustness, providing a method to resist membership inference attacks. This is a critical security aspect often overlooked by other unlearning techniques, highlighting the practical value of the research.
    *   The detailed experimental evaluation across multiple datasets, using a comprehensive suite of metrics, adds credibility to the claims. This comprehensive approach contributes to the field by providing a rigorous benchmark for evaluating LLM unlearning methods.
    *   The results demonstrate a promising balance between forget quality, model utility, and fluency, a challenging trade-off in unlearning.  The practical implications of OBLIVIATE are high, as it offers a workable solution for real-world LLM deployments.
    *   The choice of datasets is relevant, although the datasets used may not represent the full scope of what LLMs encounter in the real world. WMDP addresses a critical challenge in unlearning biosecurity and cybersecurity material that could be mis-used by the model. TOFU also provides a different dimension of unlearning tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-4o for target token extraction introduces a dependency on a proprietary and potentially unstable service. The paper acknowledges this limitation but doesn't offer a fully independent alternative.  Scalability and cost issues associated with GPT-4o for very large datasets could be a concern.
    *   While the document-level memorization metric is novel, the paper could benefit from further analysis and justification of its design choices.  A comparison with other potential metrics would strengthen the validation of DRMA.
    *  The "context-aware unlearning" argument would be enhanced by providing a more nuanced discussion of the specific prompts and settings in which selective effects are observed. Providing a table that explicitly presents these settings would aid the reader in understanding how context is incorporated into the method.
    *  The paper mentions that "selective forgetful can have limitations". The nature of the limitations is not described in the text and needs additional context,
    *  "While the method claims robustness, the real-world application on extremely large data sets and testing against new state-of-the-art attacks has not been done. This could potentially uncover new challenges".

*   **Score Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of machine unlearning for LLMs by combining existing techniques into a structured and evaluated framework.  The novel aspects (DRMA, context-aware unlearning, and GPT-4o-based target token extraction) address key challenges in LLM unlearning. While the reliance on GPT-4o and limited dataset diversity are weaknesses, the framework's practical implications, robustness claims, and comprehensive evaluation justify a high score.

**Score: 7.5**

The score is justified by the following points:

*   Clear contributions in a timely and important area of research.
*   Novelty is present, although it primarily combines existing techniques in a new structure and adds the DRMA metric.
*   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with promising results.
*   Well-written and clearly presented.
*   Addresses key practical challenges like maintaining utility, fluency, and resisting MIAs.
* The paper can be improved by explaining and validating the limitations with "selective forgetfulness"
*   Weakness in real-world datasets limits the impact of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Localized Diffusion Models for High Dimensional Distributions Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04417v1)
- **Authors**: Georg A. Gottwald, Shuigen Liu, Youssef Marzouk, Sebastian Reich, Xin T. Tong
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models are the state-of-the-art tools for various generative tasks. However, estimating high-dimensional score functions makes them potentially suffer from the curse of dimensionality (CoD). This underscores the importance of better understanding and exploiting low-dimensional structure in the target distribution. In this work, we consider locality structure, which describes sparse dependencies between model components. Under locality structure, the score function is effectively low-dimensional, so that it can be estimated by a localized neural network with significantly reduced sample complexity. This motivates the localized diffusion model, where a localized score matching loss is used to train the score function within a localized hypothesis space. We prove that such localization enables diffusion models to circumvent CoD, at the price of additional localization error. Under realistic sample size scaling, we show both theoretically and numerically that a moderate localization radius can balance the statistical and localization error, leading to a better overall performance. The localized structure also facilitates parallel training of diffusion models, making it potentially more efficient for large-scale applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper, "Localized Diffusion Models for High Dimensional Distributions Generation."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the curse of dimensionality (CoD) in diffusion models (DMs) when dealing with high-dimensional data.  The core idea is to exploit *locality* structure within the target distribution. Locality means that each component of the data has strong dependence on only a *sparse* subset of the other components. Under such structure, the authors argue and demonstrate that the score function (a crucial component of DMs) can be effectively learned with significantly reduced sample complexity by using localized neural networks. They propose the "localized diffusion model (LDM)," where the score function is trained within a localized hypothesis space using a localized score matching loss. The paper provides theoretical guarantees showing that localization enables DMs to circumvent the CoD, though at the cost of introducing localization error. The paper shows that a trade-off exists between statistical and localization error that can be balanced by adjusting the localization radius, which is further validated with numerical experiments. The authors also mention that this structure can facilitate parallel training of the DM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of leveraging locality structure to mitigate the CoD in generative modeling, and specifically within the context of diffusion models, is a valuable contribution. Existing works focus on manifold hypothesis or Gaussian mixture models for alleviating CoD, while exploiting locality is a different approach. The formalization of the localized diffusion model (LDM) and the analysis of its approximation and statistical error introduce a specific architecture and theoretical framework for this idea. While localized versions of other sampling algorithms such as MCMC exist, the adaptation to diffusion models and the accompanying analysis provide a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work lies in making DMs more practical for high-dimensional problems exhibiting locality. Many real-world datasets (e.g., spatial statistics, time series) possess such structure. If the LDM approach can significantly reduce the computational burden and sample complexity of DMs while maintaining generation quality, it could be a significant advancement. The emphasis on parallel training is also a relevant practical consideration for large-scale applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a reasonably rigorous theoretical framework, including approximation and generalization error bounds. The error decomposition provides valuable insights into the trade-offs involved in localization.
    *   **Clear Motivation:** The introduction effectively motivates the problem, explains the shortcomings of existing approaches, and clearly outlines the paper's contribution.
    *   **Numerical Validation:** The experiments on Gaussian models and the Cox-Ingersoll-Ross model provide empirical evidence supporting the theoretical claims. They demonstrate the trade-off between localization and statistical error and the importance of selecting an appropriate localization radius.
    *   **Parallelization Consideration:** Highlighting the potential for parallel training is a valuable and practically relevant point.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Assumptions:** The theoretical results rely on specific assumptions regarding the target distribution, such as log-concavity and smoothness, which may limit the applicability of the results. The degree to which these assumptions are relaxed is a point of interest for future work.
    *   **Practical Implementation Details:** The paper could benefit from more details about practical implementation challenges and guidelines for choosing the localization radius in real-world scenarios. The suggested approach, as mentioned in Remark 3.3, suggests `r* = O((log N)^(1/v))`. The logarithmic dependency on the sample size can result in a large localization radius when N is sufficiently large, which can hinder the computational efficiency.
    *   **Scope of Experiments:** The numerical experiments, while illustrative, are relatively limited in scope. Demonstrating the effectiveness of the LDM on more complex, real-world datasets would strengthen the paper's impact.
    *   **Clarity:** In some parts, the paper could be made more accessible and easier to follow, particularly in the more technical sections involving error analysis. The heavy mathematical notation might pose a challenge for some readers.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of diffusion models by introducing and analyzing a novel approach to mitigate the curse of dimensionality based on locality structure. The theoretical analysis, while relying on assumptions, provides a solid foundation for the LDM approach. The numerical experiments offer promising empirical results, although further validation on more complex datasets is needed. The consideration of parallel training is also a strength.

Score: 7

**Rationale for the Score:** A score of 7 reflects the paper's clear novelty and potential impact. It presents a new idea that can be further researched in the community and developed to be implemented and applied to new datasets in the future. The score also indicates some limitations that need to be addressed in future work, such as stronger experimental validations, as well as relaxed assumptions. The quality of the experiments is questionable (see section 4 of the paper) and might need further research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LONGER: Scaling Up Long Sequence Modeling in Industrial Recommenders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04421v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Chai, Qin Ren, Xijun Xiao, Huizhi Yang, Bo Han, Sijun Zhang, Di Chen, Hui Lu, Wenlin Zhao, Lele Yu, Xionghang Xie, Shiru Ren, Xiang Sun, Yaocheng Tan, Peng Xu, Yuchao Zheng, Di Wu
- **Abstract**: Modeling ultra-long user behavior sequences is critical for capturing both long- and short-term preferences in industrial recommender systems. Existing solutions typically rely on two-stage retrieval or indirect modeling paradigms, incuring upstream-downstream inconsistency and computational inefficiency. In this paper, we present LONGER, a Long-sequence Optimized traNsformer for GPU-Efficient Recommenders. LONGER incorporates (i) a global token mechanism for stabilizing attention over long contexts, (ii) a token merge module with lightweight InnerTransformers and hybrid attention strategy to reduce quadratic complexity, and (iii) a series of engineering optimizations, including training with mixed-precision and activation recomputation, KV cache serving, and the fully synchronous model training and serving framework for unified GPU-based dense and sparse parameter updates. LONGER consistently outperforms strong baselines in both offline metrics and online A/B testing in both advertising and e-commerce services at ByteDance, validating its consistent effectiveness and industrial-level scaling laws. Currently, LONGER has been fully deployed at more than 10 influential scenarios at ByteDance, serving billion users.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LONGER, a novel Transformer-based framework designed to handle ultra-long user behavior sequences in industrial recommender systems.  LONGER aims to address the computational challenges associated with modeling long sequences by incorporating several key innovations: (1) a global token mechanism to stabilize attention; (2) a token merge module with lightweight InnerTransformers and a hybrid attention strategy to reduce quadratic complexity; and (3) engineering optimizations for training and serving, including mixed-precision training, activation recomputation, KV cache serving, and a fully synchronous framework. The paper presents experimental results on a large-scale industrial dataset and online A/B tests, demonstrating that LONGER outperforms strong baselines in both offline metrics and online performance in advertising and e-commerce scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical Relevance:** The paper tackles a significant and pressing challenge in industrial recommender systems: efficiently modeling long user behavior sequences.  The focus on scalability and deployment makes the work highly relevant for real-world applications.
    * **Comprehensive Approach:** LONGER integrates several innovative techniques, addressing both algorithmic and systems-level aspects of long sequence modeling. The combination of global tokens, token merging, and engineering optimizations provides a holistic solution.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence of LONGER's effectiveness, using a large-scale industrial dataset and online A/B testing.  The reported performance gains over strong baselines are substantial and convincing.
    * **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies meticulously analyze the contribution of each component of LONGER, providing valuable insights into the design choices and their impact on performance.
    * **Scaling Analysis:**  The scaling analysis provides important guidance on how to optimize LONGER for different computational budgets and sequence lengths.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While LONGER combines several techniques, some of these techniques (e.g., mixed precision training, recomputation, KV caching) are well-established in the broader deep learning community. The core novelty lies in the specific way these techniques are integrated and adapted for the recommender system context. The novelty in attention mechanisms itself might not be particularly groundbreaking if considered outside the system-level design and industrial application.
    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper focuses primarily on empirical results and lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why LONGER works so well.  For example, a formal analysis of the convergence properties of the token merging strategy or the attention mechanism would strengthen the theoretical underpinnings of the work.
    * **Overclaims:** The paper sometimes tends to overstate the novelty of certain components. For instance, the KV cache serving is not unique in its application; similar techniques are used in other areas like NLP for large language models.
    * **Generalizability:** The evaluations are heavily focused on Douyin's Ads and E-commerce scenarios. While these are important real-world applications, the paper could benefit from demonstrating performance on other datasets to establish the generalizability of LONGER.

* **Significance:**
    * **Industry Impact:**  The successful deployment of LONGER at ByteDance, serving billions of users, demonstrates its significant practical value. The paper provides a blueprint for other organizations to build similar long sequence modeling systems.
    * **Community Contribution:** The paper contributes to the broader research community by demonstrating how to effectively scale long sequence models in recommender systems. The insights from the ablation studies and scaling analysis can inform future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

LONGER presents a valuable contribution to the field of industrial recommender systems.  It effectively addresses the challenge of modeling ultra-long user behavior sequences by combining algorithmic innovations with engineering optimizations. While the individual components of LONGER may not be entirely novel, their integration and adaptation for the specific context of industrial recommenders, along with rigorous empirical validation and its deployment at scale, justify a solid score.  The primary weakness lies in the incremental nature of some of the components and the lack of a deeper theoretical analysis. It makes it difficult to reach the higher ranks.

### Theoretical Guarantees for LT-TTD: A Unified Transformer-based Architecture for Two-Level Ranking Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04434v1)
- **Authors**: Ayoub Abraich
- **Abstract**: Modern recommendation and search systems typically employ multi-stage ranking architectures to efficiently handle billions of candidates. The conventional approach uses distinct L1 (candidate retrieval) and L2 (re-ranking) models with different optimization objectives, introducing critical limitations including irreversible error propagation and suboptimal ranking. This paper identifies and analyzes the fundamental limitations of this decoupled paradigm and proposes LT-TTD (Listwise Transformer with Two-Tower Distillation), a novel unified architecture that bridges retrieval and ranking phases. Our approach combines the computational efficiency of two-tower models with the expressivity of transformers in a unified listwise learning framework. We provide a comprehensive theoretical analysis of our architecture and establish formal guarantees regarding error propagation mitigation, ranking quality improvements, and optimization convergence. We derive theoretical bounds showing that LT-TTD reduces the upper limit on irretrievable relevant items by a factor that depends on the knowledge distillation strength, and prove that our multi-objective optimization framework achieves a provably better global optimum than disjoint training. Additionally, we analyze the computational complexity of our approach, demonstrating that the asymptotic complexity remains within practical bounds for real-world applications. We also introduce UPQE, a novel evaluation metric specifically designed for unified ranking architectures that holistically captures retrieval quality, ranking performance, and computational efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Theoretical Guarantees for LT-TTD: A Unified Transformer-based Architecture for Two-Level Ranking Systems" addresses limitations in traditional two-level ranking systems (L1 retrieval followed by L2 re-ranking). It proposes a novel architecture called LT-TTD (Listwise Transformer with Two-Tower Distillation) which unifies retrieval and ranking. LT-TTD combines the efficiency of two-tower models with the expressiveness of transformers in a listwise learning framework. The paper offers a comprehensive theoretical analysis, including error propagation mitigation, ranking quality improvements, optimization convergence, and computational complexity. It also introduces a new evaluation metric, UPQE, for unified ranking architectures. The authors provide theoretical guarantees in the form of theorems and proofs demonstrating the benefits of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of unifying the two stages in ranking and mitigating error propagation with a knowledge distillation approach possesses some novelty. Traditional ranking systems often treat the two stages as distinct entities, leading to the problems the authors identify. The use of a Listwise Transformer combined with Two-Tower architecture and knowledge distillation to bridge the gap is a relatively new angle. Prior work has primarily focused on improving each stage independently rather than unifying them. However, the individual components (two-tower, transformers, knowledge distillation) are established techniques, so the novelty lies primarily in their specific combination and the theoretical guarantees provided.

*   **Significance:** If the theoretical guarantees translate into practical improvements, the work has the potential to significantly influence the design of large-scale ranking systems. The analysis of error propagation and optimization conflicts in cascaded systems provides valuable insights. The proposed UPQE metric, while theoretical, offers a more comprehensive way to evaluate such integrated systems. This is particularly significant given the widespread use of two-level ranking in industry. If this paper proves that the suggested approach mitigates the pitfalls of two-level architectures, it carries significant relevance. However, the paper would have been more impactful if it included experiments that validate the theoretical findings empirically.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper presents rigorous mathematical analysis and provides theoretical bounds for several aspects of its approach, including error propagation reduction and optimization convergence. This distinguishes it from many papers that rely solely on empirical evaluation.
    *   **Identified Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of traditional two-level ranking systems. The formalization of error propagation and optimization conflicts highlights areas for improvement.
    *   **Unified Architecture:** The proposed LT-TTD architecture offers a promising way to address the identified limitations while maintaining computational efficiency.
    *   **New Metric:** UPQE introduces a relevant metric for assessing unified systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The most significant weakness is the absence of experimental results. The theoretical guarantees are compelling but need to be validated through experiments on real-world datasets. Without empirical evidence, it is hard to assess the practical impact and scalability of LT-TTD.
    *   **Complexity:** The architecture is complex with three major interacting components. While the paper addresses complexity, practical deployments may face challenges in tuning, optimization, and resource allocation.
    *   **Assumptions:** The theoretical guarantees rely on several assumptions about the loss functions and optimization process. The validity of these assumptions in real-world scenarios needs to be investigated.
    *   **Limited novelty:** Despite the combination being new, the individual components are not novel.

*   **Potential Influence:** If empirically validated, LT-TTD could become a foundational architecture for the next generation of ranking systems. The UPQE metric can influence how such systems are evaluated.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper's strong theoretical foundation and problem articulation are commendable, but the lack of empirical evidence significantly weakens its impact. The theoretical contributions are valuable, but their practical relevance remains unproven. Without experiments, we don't know if the gains predicted by the theorems are significant in practice or if the assumptions hold. The approach also requires significant engineering effort to correctly implement and integrate the different modules effectively. Also, while the concept of unified architecture is interesting, the individual modules are not novel, and novelty lies mainly in their integration. Taking all the points into account, I believe a score of 6 reflects the paper's strengths and weaknesses fairly.

### Towards Effectively Leveraging Execution Traces for Program Repair with Code LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04441v1)
- **Authors**: Mirazul Haque, Petr Babkin, Farima Farmahinifarahani, Manuela Veloso
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) show promising performance on various programming tasks, including Automatic Program Repair (APR). However, most approaches to LLM-based APR are limited to the static analysis of the programs, while disregarding their runtime behavior. Inspired by knowledge-augmented NLP, in this work, we aim to remedy this potential blind spot by augmenting standard APR prompts with program execution traces. We evaluate our approach using the GPT family of models on three popular APR datasets. Our findings suggest that simply incorporating execution traces into the prompt provides a limited performance improvement over trace-free baselines, in only 2 out of 6 tested dataset / model configurations. We further find that the effectiveness of execution traces for APR diminishes as their complexity increases. We explore several strategies for leveraging traces in prompts and demonstrate that LLM-optimized prompts help outperform trace-free prompts more consistently. Additionally, we show trace-based prompting to be superior to finetuning a smaller LLM on a small-scale dataset; and conduct probing studies reinforcing the notion that execution traces can complement the reasoning abilities of the LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of incorporating program execution traces into prompts for LLM-based automatic program repair (APR).  It compares trace-augmented prompts against trace-free baselines (including a chain-of-thought "self-debug" approach) using GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 on three datasets. The study finds that simply adding execution traces to prompts doesn't consistently improve performance. Further analysis reveals that the complexity of traces (length, variable modifications) negatively impacts effectiveness. The paper then explores strategies to optimize trace usage within prompts, including collated traces, LLM-optimized traces (OPT), and confidence-based/trace-length-based prompt selection.  LLM-optimized traces are found to provide the most consistent improvements.  Finally, the paper compares trace-augmented prompting with fine-tuning a smaller LLM and investigates LLM capabilities in trace-related tasks (trace collating and prediction).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using execution traces to augment LLM prompts for APR is logical and builds upon prior work in knowledge-augmented NLP.  Several works have already looked into using execution traces for pre-training. The *novelty lies in the systematic exploration of different trace incorporation strategies within prompts and the analysis of trace complexity.*  The comparison with fine-tuning a smaller model also adds a useful dimension, although it's not entirely unique. The trace collating and prediction experiments provide valuable insights into the capabilities of LLMs regarding traces.

*   **Significance:** APR is an important area, and LLMs are showing promise. *Understanding how to best leverage dynamic information like execution traces to enhance LLM-based APR is a worthwhile pursuit.* The paper provides a solid empirical analysis of different prompting strategies. The finding that trace complexity can be detrimental is important and suggests directions for future research. The results regarding the limitations of fine-tuning a smaller LLM versus prompting are also helpful for the community. The probing studies provide some qualitative insights, but could benefit from being more in-depth.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper conducts a reasonably thorough evaluation of different prompting strategies across multiple datasets and models.
    *   **Analysis of Trace Complexity:** The investigation into the impact of trace length and variable modifications is a valuable contribution.
    *   **LLM-Optimized Traces:** The idea of using an LLM to optimize traces for downstream APR is interesting.
    *   **Comparison with Fine-Tuning:** The comparison with fine-tuning a smaller model is a useful sanity check and provides practical guidance.
    *   **Probing Studies:** The tracing and alignment probing experiments, though preliminary, are a plus.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Contribution:**  While systematic, the core idea of adding execution traces isn't groundbreaking. The improvements reported are often modest.
    *   **Limited Depth in Qualitative Analysis:**  The paper could benefit from more in-depth qualitative analysis of why certain prompting strategies work better than others. The probing studies lacked in-depth insights.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** While using algorithmic datasets has advantages, generalizing results to more complex, real-world software is a potential concern.
    *   **Limited Model Choice:** Only using two models from the GPT family is limited. Evaluating additional, especially open-source models, would add more value.
    *   **The description of some techniques could be more detailed.** Some prompting strategies (such as the confidence-based approach) might benefit from a more detailed explanation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence future research in LLM-based APR by encouraging a more nuanced approach to incorporating dynamic information. The results regarding trace complexity and LLM-optimized traces could lead to the development of more effective APR systems.
    The tracing & collating experiments are useful in showing that there is some value in using traces.

**Overall:** The paper provides a solid, well-executed empirical analysis of trace-augmented prompting for APR. While the core idea isn't revolutionary, the systematic evaluation, the analysis of trace complexity, and the LLM-optimized trace strategy are valuable contributions. It would be helpful to see more qualitative analysis and use additional models in further experiments.

### M2Rec: Multi-scale Mamba for Efficient Sequential Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04445v1)
- **Authors**: Qianru Zhang, Liang Qu, Honggang Wen, Dong Huang, Siu-Ming Yiu, Nguyen Quoc Viet Hung, Hongzhi Yin
- **Abstract**: Sequential recommendation systems aim to predict users' next preferences based on their interaction histories, but existing approaches face critical limitations in efficiency and multi-scale pattern recognition. While Transformer-based methods struggle with quadratic computational complexity, recent Mamba-based models improve efficiency but fail to capture periodic user behaviors, leverage rich semantic information, or effectively fuse multimodal features. To address these challenges, we propose \model, a novel sequential recommendation framework that integrates multi-scale Mamba with Fourier analysis, Large Language Models (LLMs), and adaptive gating. First, we enhance Mamba with Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) to explicitly model periodic patterns in the frequency domain, separating meaningful trends from noise. Second, we incorporate LLM-based text embeddings to enrich sparse interaction data with semantic context from item descriptions. Finally, we introduce a learnable gate mechanism to dynamically balance temporal (Mamba), frequency (FFT), and semantic (LLM) features, ensuring harmonious multimodal fusion. Extensive experiments demonstrate that \model\ achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving Hit Rate@10 by 3.2\% over existing Mamba-based models while maintaining 20\% faster inference than Transformer baselines. Our results highlight the effectiveness of combining frequency analysis, semantic understanding, and adaptive fusion for sequential recommendation. Code and datasets are available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/M2Rec.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper, including a score and justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "M²Rec: Multi-scale Mamba for Efficient Sequential Recommendation" introduces a novel sequential recommendation framework built upon the Mamba architecture.  It addresses limitations of existing sequential recommendation models by incorporating three key components: (1) Enhanced Mamba with Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) to explicitly model and separate periodic patterns from noise in user interactions, (2) LLM-based text embeddings to enrich sparse interaction data with semantic context from item descriptions, and (3) a learnable gate mechanism to dynamically balance the contributions of temporal (Mamba), frequency (FFT), and semantic (LLM) features.  The authors demonstrate through extensive experiments that M²Rec achieves state-of-the-art performance and better efficiency, surpassing existing Mamba-based models and maintaining comparable efficiency to Transformer-based models while offering improvements in hit ratio and inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The paper's novelty lies in the *integration* of three well-established techniques (Mamba, FFT, and LLMs) into a cohesive framework for sequential recommendation. While each component individually is not new, the synergistic combination, especially with the adaptive gating mechanism, and its application to sequential recommendation, is a distinct contribution. The use of FFT to explicitly model periodic patterns in *frequency space* before feeding it into the Mamba architecture is a novel idea. Many models attempt to implicitly learn periodicities; this approach makes it explicit.
    *  The adaptive gating mechanism also presents a novel way of blending temporal dynamics (Mamba), frequency patterns (FFT), and semantic information (LLMs) within the context of sequential recommendations, which leads to a balanced and effective fusion of multimodal information.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper tackles important challenges in sequential recommendation: efficiency, especially with long sequences, and the effective capture of multi-scale user behaviors. The Mamba architecture offers a path towards more scalable solutions compared to traditional Transformers.
    *   The explicit modeling of periodicity is significant because user behavior often exhibits predictable patterns (daily, weekly, seasonal). Accurately capturing these patterns can lead to better recommendations.  The use of LLMs to enrich sparse interaction data addresses the cold-start problem and improves the representation of items.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing state-of-the-art methods. This is a significant finding that will encourage further research in this direction.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach.
    *   The motivation is well-articulated, and the problem addressed is relevant to the field.
    *   The integration of different techniques is well-justified.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough and uses multiple benchmark datasets.
    *   The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   The efficiency analysis demonstrates the practicality of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual components (Mamba, FFT, LLMs) are not entirely novel. The paper's primary contribution is in the *orchestration* of these elements.
    *   While the paper highlights a performance improvement over existing methods, it does not explain the complex interplay between different time windows or offer a more detailed analysis of their performance.
    *   The parameter settings are not described in great detail. Some additional details on parameter tuning and architecture choices might enhance reproducibility and provide a better understanding of the model's behavior.
    *   The approach still seems rather complex, which may limit its adoption. Simpler architectures that achieve comparable performance are often favored in practice.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to influence future research in sequential recommendation by encouraging the exploration of Mamba-based models and the integration of frequency analysis techniques.
    *   The adaptive gating mechanism could be adopted in other multimodal recommendation systems.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of sequential recommendation by combining Mamba, FFT, and LLMs in a novel and effective way. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the integration is well-motivated, and the experimental results demonstrate clear improvements over existing methods. The weaknesses (complexity and a degree of reliance on well-established building blocks) prevent a truly exceptional score.
